             'src="./Steele-olszewski-richardson_disease.jpg" '
             'width="280"/></span></td></tr><tr><td class="infobox-full-data" colspan="2">MRI '
             'demonstrating the hummingbird sign of supranuclear palsy due to atrophy of the '
             'midbrain</td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" scope="row"><a '
             'href="Medical_specialty" title="Medical specialty">Specialty</a></th><td '
             'class="infobox-data"><a href="Neurology" '
             'title="Neurology">Neurology</a></td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" '
             'scope="row"><a href="Signs_and_symptoms" title="Signs and '
             'symptoms">Symptoms</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><div '
             'class="hlist"><ul><li>Impaired balance</li><li><a href="Hypokinesia" '
             'title="Hypokinesia">slowed movements</a></li><li><a href="Ophthalmoparesis" '
             'title="Ophthalmoparesis">difficulty moving eyes</a></li><li><a href="Dementia" '
             'title="Dementia">dementia</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" '
             'scope="row">Usual onset</th><td class="infobox-data">60–70 years</td></tr><tr><th '
             'class="infobox-label" scope="row"><a href="Cause_(medicine)" title="Cause '
             '(medicine)">Causes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Unknown</td></tr><tr><th '
             'class="infobox-label" scope="row"><a href="Differential_diagnosis" '
             'title="Differential diagnosis">Differential diagnosis</a></th><td '
             'class="infobox-data"><div class="hlist"><ul><li><a href="Parkinson\'s_disease" '
             'title="Parkinson\'s disease">Parkinson\'s disease</a></li><li><a '
             'href="Corticobasal_degeneration" title="Corticobasal degeneration">corticobasal '
             'degeneration</a></li><li><a class="mw-redirect" href="FTDP-17" '
             'title="FTDP-17">FTDP-17</a></li><li><a href="Alzheimer\'s_disease" '
             'title="Alzheimer\'s disease">Alzheimer\'s '
             'disease</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" '
             'scope="row">Treatment</th><td class="infobox-data"><div '
             'class="hlist"><ul><li>Medication</li><li>physical therapy</li><li>occupational '
             'therapy</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" scope="row"><a '
             'href="Medication" title="Medication">Medication</a></th><td '
             'class="infobox-data"><div class="hlist"><ul><li><a href="L-DOPA" '
             'title="L-DOPA">Levodopa</a></li><li><a href="Amantadine" '
             'title="Amantadine">amantadine</a></li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th '
             'class="infobox-label" scope="row"><a href="Prognosis" '
             'title="Prognosis">Prognosis</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Fatal (usually 7–10 '
             'years after diagnosis)</td></tr><tr><th class="infobox-label" '
             'scope="row">Frequency</th><td class="infobox-data">6 per '
             '100,000</td></tr></tbody></table> <p><b>Progressive supranuclear palsy</b> '
             '(<b>PSP</b>) is a late-onset <a href="Neurodegenerative_disease" '
             'title="Neurodegenerative disease">neurodegenerative disease</a> involving the '
             'gradual deterioration and death of specific volumes of the <a href="Brain" '
             'title="Brain">brain</a>, linked to 4-repeat tau pathology. The condition leads to '
             'symptoms including <a href="Balance_disorder" title="Balance disorder">loss of '
             'balance</a>, <a href="Hypokinesia" title="Hypokinesia">slowing of movement</a>, <a '
             'href="Ophthalmoparesis" title="Ophthalmoparesis">difficulty moving the eyes</a>, and '
             '<a href="Cognitive_impairment" title="Cognitive impairment">cognitive '
             'impairment</a>. PSP may be mistaken for other types of neurodegeneration such as <a '
             'href="Parkinson\'s_disease" title="Parkinson\'s disease">Parkinson\'s disease</a>, '
             '<a href="Frontotemporal_dementia" title="Frontotemporal dementia">frontotemporal '
             'dementia</a> and <a href="Alzheimer\'s_disease" title="Alzheimer\'s '
             'disease">Alzheimer\'s disease</a>. It is the second most common tauopathy behind <a '
             'href="Alzheimer\'s_disease" title="Alzheimer\'s disease">Alzheimer\'s disease</a>. '
             'The cause of the condition is uncertain, but involves the accumulation of tau '
             'protein within the brain. Medications such as <a href="L-DOPA" '
             'title="L-DOPA">levodopa</a> and <a href="Amantadine" '
             'title="Amantadine">amantadine</a> may be useful in some cases. </p><p>PSP was first '
             'officially described by Richardson, Steele, and Olszewski in 1963 as a form of '
             'progressive parkinsonism. However, the earliest known case presenting clinical '
             'features consistent with PSP, along with pathological confirmation, was reported in '
             'France in 1951. Originally thought to be a more general type of atypical '
             'parkinsonism, PSP is now linked to distinct clinical phenotypes including '
             "<b>PSP-Richardson's syndrome (PSP-RS)</b>, which is the most common sub-type of the "
             'disease. As PSP advances to a fully symptomatic stage, many PSP subtypes eventually '
             'exhibit the clinical characteristics of PSP-RS. </p><p>PSP, encompassing all its '
             'phenotypes, has a prevalence of 18 per 100,000, whereas PSP-RS affects approximately '
             '5 to 7 per 100,000 individuals. The first symptoms typically occur at 60–70 years of '
             'age. Males are slightly more likely to be affected than females. No association has '
             'been found between PSP and any particular race, location, or occupation. </p> <meta '
             'property="mw:PageProp/toc"/> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 '
             'id="Signs_and_symptoms">Signs and symptoms</h2></div> <p>The initial symptoms in '
             'two-thirds of cases are loss of balance, lunging forward when mobilizing, fast '
             'walking, bumping into objects or people, and . <a href="Dementia" '
             'title="Dementia">Dementia</a> symptoms are also initially seen in about one in five '
             'cases. </p><p>Other common early symptoms are changes in personality, general '
             'slowing of movement, and visual symptoms. The most common behavioural symptoms in '
             'patients with PSP include apathy, <a href="Disinhibition" title="Disinhibition">a '
             'lack of inhibition</a>, <a href="Anxiety" title="Anxiety">anxiety</a>, and a '
             'profound state of unease or dissatisfaction. </p><p>Later symptoms and signs can '
             'include, but do not necessarily include dementia (typically including loss of '
             'inhibition and ability to organize information), <a href="Dysarthria" '
             'title="Dysarthria">slurring of speech</a>, <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Difficulty_swallowing" title="Difficulty swallowing">difficulty '
             'swallowing</a>, and difficulty <a class="mw-redirect" href="Eye_movement_(sensory)" '
             'title="Eye movement (sensory)">moving the eyes</a>, particularly in the vertical '
             'direction. The latter accounts for some of the falls experienced by these patients, '
             'as they find it difficult to look up or down. </p><p>Some of the other signs are '
             'poor <a href="Eyelid" title="Eyelid">eyelid</a> function, <a href="Contracture" '
             'title="Contracture">contracture</a> of the <a href="Facial_muscles" title="Facial '
             'muscles">facial muscles</a>, a backward tilt of the head with stiffening of the <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Table_of_muscles_of_the_human_body%3A_Neck" title="Table '
             'of muscles of the human body: Neck">neck muscles</a>, <a href="Sleep_disorder" '
             'title="Sleep disorder">sleep disruption</a>, <a href="Urinary_incontinence" '
             'title="Urinary incontinence">urinary incontinence</a>, and <a href="Constipation" '
             'title="Constipation">constipation</a>. Some patients retain full cognitive function '
             'up to the end. </p><p>The visual symptoms are of particular importance in the '
             'diagnosis of this disorder. Patients typically complain of difficulty reading due to '
             'the inability to look downwards. The <a href="Ophthalmoparesis" '
             'title="Ophthalmoparesis">ophthalmoparesis</a> experienced by these patients mainly '
             'concerns voluntary eye movement and the inability to make vertical <a href="Saccade" '
             'title="Saccade">saccades</a>, which is often worse with downward saccades. Patients '
             'tend to have difficulty looking down (a downgaze <a class="extiw external" '
             'href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/palsy" title="wikt:palsy">palsy</a>) followed '
             'by the addition of an upgaze palsy. This vertical gaze <a href="Paresis" '
             'title="Paresis">paresis</a> will correct when the examiner passively rolls the '
             'patient\'s head up and down as part of a test for the <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Oculocephalic_reflex" title="Oculocephalic reflex">oculocephalic reflex</a>. '
             'Involuntary eye movement, as elicited by <a href="Bell\'s_phenomenon" title="Bell\'s '
             'phenomenon">Bell\'s phenomenon</a>, for instance, may be closer to normal. </p><p>On '
             'close inspection, eye movements called "square-wave jerks" may be visible when the '
             'patient fixes gaze at distance. These are fine movements, that can be mistaken for '
             '<a href="Nystagmus" title="Nystagmus">nystagmus</a>, except that they are saccadic '
             'in nature, with no smooth phase. Although healthy individuals also make square-wave '
             'jerk movements, PSP patients make slower square-wave jerk movements, with smaller '
             'vertical components. Assessment of these square-wave jerks and diminished vertical '
             'saccades is especially useful for diagnosing progressive supranuclear palsy, because '
             'these movements set PSP patients apart from other parkinsonian patients. '
             'Difficulties with <a class="mw-redirect" href="Convergence_(eye)" title="Convergence '
             '(eye)">convergence</a> (convergence insufficiency), where the eyes come closer '
             'together while focusing on something near, like the pages of a book, is typical. '
             'Because the eyes have trouble coming together to focus at short distances, the '
             'patient may complain of <a href="Diplopia" title="Diplopia">diplopia</a> (double '
             'vision) when reading. </p><p>A characteristic facial appearance known as <a '
             'href="Procerus_sign" title="Procerus sign">procerus sign</a>, with a wide-eye stare, '
             'furrowing of forehead with a frowning expression, and deepening of other facial '
             'creases, is also diagnostic of PSP. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="Signs_and_symptoms_of_PSP-RS_subtype">Signs and symptoms of PSP-RS '
             'subtype</h3></div> <p>PSP-RS is characterized by a combination of motor, ocular, '
             'cognitive, and speech-related impairments, that typically emerge in early stages of '
             'the disease. Symptoms of PSP-RS usually begin after 60 and steadily progress over '
             'time. Clinical symptoms of PSP-RS often include unexplained falls, unsteady gait, '
             'bradykinesia, apathy, disinhibition, cognitive dysfunction, difficulty planning or '
             'multitasking, slow speech, and impaired ocular movement. PSP-RS is also '
             'characterized by unresponsiveness to dopamine therapies often prescribed for those '
             'with <a href="Parkinson\'s_disease" title="Parkinson\'s disease">Parkinson\'s '
             'disease</a>. Patients can present initially with symptoms more characteristic of the '
             'PSP-Parkinson (PSP-P) subtype which is characterized by asymmetric rigidity, resting '
             'tremor, and are more responsive to dopamine therapies such as <a href="Levodopa" '
             'title="Levodopa">levadopa</a> compared to PSP-RS. Clinical and pathological '
             'differences between PSP-P and PRSP-RS occur within the first year of the disease, '
             'with individuals with PSP-RS exhibiting faster progression of symptoms and lower '
             'survival rates after diagnosis. Diagnostic criteria distinguish between '
             '<b>probable</b> and <b>possible</b> PSP-RS, as definitive diagnosis requires '
             'post-mortem neuropathological confirmation. The NINDS-SPSP criteria define probable '
             'PSP-RS as requiring both vertical supranuclear gaze palsy and early postural '
             'instability with falls, while possible PSP-RS requires either vertical gaze palsy or '
             'slowed vertical saccades combined with early falls. PSP-RS effects more males than '
             'females, with a male to female ratio of 1.8:1. Symptom progression tends to occur '
             'more quickly in PSP-RS than other sub-types with the average disease duration being '
             '5.9 years and an average age of death of 72.1 years. </p> <div class="mw-heading '
             'mw-heading4"><h4 id="Motor_symptoms_in_PSP-RS">Motor symptoms in PSP-RS</h4></div> '
             '<p>One of the key features of PSP-RS that occurs within the first year of symptom '
             'onset, is early postural instability which often leads to unexplained falls. '
             'Additionally, patients present with axial rigidity, which is characterized by '
             'stiffness in the neck and body. This is often accompanied with <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Bradykinesia" title="Bradykinesia">bradykinesia</a> which '
             "is slowness of movement. Although PSP-RS is often misdiagnosed as Parkinson's "
             'disease, tremors are uncommon in PSP-RS. Motor symptoms are often symmetrical in '
             'PSP-RS with both sides of the body being affected. </p> <div class="mw-heading '
             'mw-heading4"><h4 id="Ocular_symptoms_in_PSP-RS">Ocular symptoms in PSP-RS</h4></div> '
             '<p>A defining feature of PSP-RS is vertical supranuclear gaze palsy, which is '
             'difficulty with voluntary downward gaze. Vertical supranuclear gaze palsy, a symptom '
             'characterized by decreased velocity and amplitude of vertical eye movements '
             '(saccades) is often the prominent diagnostic feature of PSP-RS. Approximately 40% of '
             'patients with PSP-RS experiencing supranuclear gaze palsy, but it may not present '
             'until 3–4 years after disease onset. Individuals with PSP-RS also display other '
             'ocular motor symptoms such as dry, red and sore eyes, blurred vision, and difficulty '
             'focusing. They may also experience spontaneous and involuntary eye-lid closure or '
             'apraxia of the eyelid opening. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 '
             'id="Cognitive_and_behavioral_symptoms_in_PSP-RS">Cognitive and behavioral symptoms '
             'in PSP-RS</h4></div> <p>Cognitive changes are frequent in PSP-RS compared to other '
             'PSP subtypes and include slowed thinking (bradyphrenia), executive dysfunction, and '
             'difficulty with planning or problem-solving. Some patients exhibit apathy, emotional '
             'blunting, or involuntary episodes of laughing or crying unrelated to mood. Some '
             'studies report that around half the individuals with PSP-RS develop these '
             'personality changes within 2-years of diagnosis. Dementia is not typically a '
             'dominant feature early on but may develop in later stages. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Cause">Cause</h2></div> <p>The cause of PSP '
             'is unknown. Fewer than 1% of those with PSP have a family member with the same '
             'disorder. A variant in the MAPT gene for tau protein called the <i>H1</i> haplotype, '
             'located on <a class="mw-redirect" href="Chromosome_17_(human)" title="Chromosome 17 '
             '(human)">chromosome 17</a> (<a class="external text" '
             'href="https://www.snpedia.com/index.php/Rs1800547" rel="nofollow">rs1800547</a>), '
             'has been linked to PSP. Nearly all people with PSP received a copy of that variant '
             'from each parent, but this is true of about two-thirds of the general population. '
             'The H1 haplotype of the <i>MAPT</i> gene has been identified in approximately 94% of '
             'individuals with PSP, compared to around 78% in healthy adults. Therefore, the '
             '<i>H1</i> haplotype appears to be necessary but not sufficient to cause PSP. Other '
             'genes, as well as <a href="Pollution" title="Pollution">environmental toxins</a>, '
             'are being investigated as other possible contributors to the cause of PSP. '
             '</p><p>Additionally, the <i>H2</i> haplotype, combined with vascular dysfunction, '
             'seems to be a factor of vascular progressive supranuclear palsy. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Genetic_mechanisms_in_PSP-RS">Genetic '
             'mechanisms in PSP-RS</h3></div> <p>Although there is a strong correlation between '
             'the H1 haplotype and PSP, the exact molecular mechanism remains unclear. Some '
             'individuals with PSP-RS have reported a family history of the disease, suggesting '
             'the possibility that genetic factors, such as the H1 haplotype, may contribute to '
             'inherited susceptibility in certain cases. The PSP-RS subtype is known for a buildup '
             'of the 4R tau protein, which does not dissolve properly and forms insoluble '
             'aggregates in the brain. In healthy brains, there is typically a balanced ratio of '
             '3-repeat (3R) and 4-repeat (4R) tau isoforms, resulting from the regulated inclusion '
             'or exclusion of exon 10 on the <i>MAPT</i> gene. Inclusion of exon 10 promotes the '
             'production of 4R tau, while its exclusion favors 3R tau. In PSP-RS, this balance is '
             'disrupted. Certain genetic variations in MAPT, most notably the H1/H1 haplotype, are '
             'associated with increased inclusion of exon 10, leading to elevated levels of 4R tau '
             'relative to 3R tau. The resulting overproduction of 4R tau is believed to contribute '
             'to the pathological accumulation of tau aggregates observed in PSP-RS. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Risk_factors">Risk factors</h3></div> <p>Risk '
             'factors for PSP are still being explored, but research has begun to uncover '
             'potential genetic, environmental, and biological contributors that may increase the '
             'likelihood of developing this condition. Associations between <a href="Hypertension" '
             'title="Hypertension">hypertension</a> and increased risk for PSP development have '
             'been uncovered. Additionally, ties to <a href="Cerebrovascular_disease" '
             'title="Cerebrovascular disease">cerebrovascular disease</a> and <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Diabetes_mellitus" title="Diabetes mellitus">diabetes '
             'mellitus</a> has been discovered, with type 2 diabetes being associated with '
             'increased brain atrophy and neurodegeneration. Cerebrovascular disease may increase '
             'risk of vascular pathology and enhance disease burden through decreased blood flow '
             'and secondary injury. </p><p>Besides <a href="Tauopathy" '
             'title="Tauopathy">tauopathy</a>, <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Mitochondrial_dysfunction" title="Mitochondrial dysfunction">mitochondrial '
             'dysfunction</a> seems to be a factor involved in PSP. Especially, <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Mitochondrial_complex_I" title="Mitochondrial complex '
             'I">mitochondrial complex I</a> inhibitors (such as acetogenins and quinolines '
             'contained in <i>Annonaceae</i> plants, as well as rotenoids) are implicated in '
             'PSP-like brain injuries. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 '
             'id="Pathophysiology">Pathophysiology</h2></div> <figure class="mw-default-size" '
             'typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><img class="mw-file-element" data-file-height="495" '
             'data-file-width="450" decoding="async" height="275" loading="lazy" '
             'src="./Taupathy.svg.png" width="250"/><figcaption>The image illustrates abnormal '
             'aggregation of 4-repeat (4R) tau protein (blue) disrupting microtubule structures '
             '(red), which impairs neuronal stability and function. Tau accumulation is a key '
             'pathological hallmark of PSP-RS and contributes to '
             'neurodegeneration.</figcaption></figure> <p>The affected brain cells are both <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Neurons" title="Neurons">neurons</a> and <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Glial_cells" title="Glial cells">glial cells</a>. The '
             'neurons display <a class="mw-redirect" href="Neurofibrillary_tangles" '
             'title="Neurofibrillary tangles">neurofibrillary tangles</a> (NFTs), which are clumps '
             "of tau protein, a normal part of a brain cell's internal structural skeleton. These "
             "tangles are often different from those seen in Alzheimer's disease, but may be "
             'structurally similar when they occur in the cerebral cortex. Their chemical '
             'composition is usually different, however, and is similar to that of tangles seen in '
             '<a href="Corticobasal_degeneration" title="Corticobasal degeneration">corticobasal '
             'degeneration</a>. Individuals with PSP typically exhibit reduced levels of total tau '
             'and phosphorylated tau in CSF compared to the elevated levels observed in '
             'Alzheimer’s disease, but these levels are still higher than those found in healthy '
             'controls. One biomarkers for PSP is <b>neurofilament light chain (NfL)</b>, a '
             'non-specific marker of axonal damage. NfL levels in the CSF of individuals with PSP '
             'are reported to be two to five times higher than those in healthy individuals or '
             'patients with Parkinson’s disease, <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Parkinson\'s_dementia" title="Parkinson\'s dementia">Parkinson’s dementia</a>, '
             'or <a href="Dementia_with_Lewy_bodies" title="Dementia with Lewy bodies">dementia '
             'with Lewy bodies</a>. However, NfL levels are similar in PSP and other disorders '
             'like <a href="Corticobasal_syndrome" title="Corticobasal syndrome">corticobasal '
             'syndrome</a> (CBS) and MSA, limiting its utility for differential diagnosis among '
             'these conditions. Still, NfL might help track how PSP gets worse over time. '
             'Blood-based measurements of NfL have also shown promise, with plasma levels strongly '
             'correlating with CSF concentrations. </p> <figure class="mw-default-size" '
             'typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><img class="mw-file-element" data-file-height="1542" '
             'data-file-width="2080" decoding="async" height="185" loading="lazy" '
             'src="./PSP_Tau_tufted_astrocyte.jpg" width="250"/><figcaption>Immunohistochemical '
             'image of a tau-positive tufted astrocyte in a brain sample from a patient with '
             'PSP-RS. Tufted astrocytes are a pathological hallmark of PSP and are characterized '
             'by abnormal accumulation of 4-repeat (4R) tau protein within astrocytic '
             'processes.</figcaption></figure> <p>Tufts of tau protein in <a href="Astrocyte" '
             'title="Astrocyte">astrocytes</a>, or tufted astrocytes, are also considered '
             'diagnostic. <b>Tufted astrocytes</b> are astrocytes that accumulate abnormally '
             'phosphorylated 4R tau in their proximal processes. These astrocytic inclusions are '
             'considered a key distinguishing pathological marker of PSP-RS and are particularly '
             'concentrated in affected subcortical regions. PSP is specifically associated with an '
             'increase in the deposition of 4R tau proteins, which differ from other tauopathies '
             'such as Alzheimer’s disease, where both 3-repeat and 4-repeat tau are present. '
             'Unlike globose NFTs, tau depositions may be more widespread in the cortex. <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Lewy_bodies" title="Lewy bodies">Lewy bodies</a> are seen '
             'in some cases, but whether this is a variant or an independent co-existing process '
             'is not clear, and in some cases, PSP can coexist with <a '
             'href="Corticobasal_degeneration" title="Corticobasal degeneration">corticobasal '
             'degeneration</a>, Parkinson\'s, and/or <a href="Alzheimer\'s_disease" '
             'title="Alzheimer\'s disease">Alzheimer\'s disease</a>, particularly with older '
             'patients. Additional pathological features include oligodendroglial coiled bodies, '
             'neuronal loss, and gliosis. These changes contribute to the characteristic symptoms '
             'of the disease, such as postural instability (from midbrain atrophy), gaze palsy '
             '(due to involvement of vertical gaze centers), and cognitive decline (from frontal '
             'lobe degeneration). </p><p>The principal areas of the brain affected are the: </p> '
             '<ul><li><a href="Basal_ganglia" title="Basal ganglia">basal ganglia</a>, '
             'particularly the <a href="Subthalamic_nucleus" title="Subthalamic '
             'nucleus">subthalamic nucleus</a>, <a href="Substantia_nigra" title="Substantia '
             'nigra">substantia nigra</a>, and <a href="Globus_pallidus" title="Globus '
             'pallidus">globus pallidus</a></li> <li><a href="Brainstem" '
             'title="Brainstem">brainstem</a>, particularly the <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Tectum" title="Tectum">tectum</a> (the portion of the midbrain where '
             '"supranuclear" eye movement resides), as well as <a href="Dopaminergic" '
             'title="Dopaminergic">dopaminergic</a> nuclei</li> <li><a href="Cerebral_cortex" '
             'title="Cerebral cortex">cerebral cortex</a>, particularly that of the <a '
             'href="Frontal_lobe" title="Frontal lobe">frontal lobes</a> and the <a '
             'href="Limbic_system" title="Limbic system">limbic system</a> (similarly to <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Frontotemporal_degeneration" title="Frontotemporal '
             'degeneration">frontotemporal degeneration</a>)</li> <li><a href="Dentate_nucleus" '
             'title="Dentate nucleus">dentate nucleus</a> of the <a href="Cerebellum" '
             'title="Cerebellum">cerebellum</a></li> <li><a href="Spinal_cord" title="Spinal '
             'cord">spinal cord</a>, particularly the area where some control of the bladder and '
             'bowel resides</li></ul> <p>The progression of tau pathology in PSP is thought to '
             'follow a pattern that begins in the striatum and advances to the frontal and '
             'parietal lobes, then to the temporal and occipital lobes, and ultimately the '
             'brainstem. </p><p>Some consider PSP, <a href="Corticobasal_degeneration" '
             'title="Corticobasal degeneration">corticobasal degeneration</a>, and <a '
             'href="Frontotemporal_dementia" title="Frontotemporal dementia">frontotemporal '
             'dementia</a> (especially <a class="mw-redirect" href="FTDP-17" '
             'title="FTDP-17">FTDP-17</a>) to be variations of the same disease. Others consider '
             'them separate diseases. PSP has been shown occasionally to co-exist with <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Pick\'s_disease" title="Pick\'s disease">Pick\'s '
             'disease</a>. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="Pathophysiology_in_PSP-RS_subtypes">Pathophysiology in PSP-RS '
             'subtypes</h3></div> <p>Tau pathology in PSP-RS is generally more severe than in '
             'other subtypes and frequently involves regions such as the basal ganglia, '
             'subthalamic nucleus, tectum, locus coeruleus, and dentate nucleus. These differences '
             'are captured by the PSP-tau score, a semiquantitative 12-point scale used to assess '
             'the extent and distribution of tau pathology, with scores ranging from 0 (minimal '
             'involvement) to 12 (widespread and severe involvement). While individuals with PSP-P '
             'typically have PSP-tau scores greater than 5, PSP-RS cases often present with higher '
             'scores, reflecting a greater overall tau burden in this subtype. </p> <figure '
             'class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><img class="mw-file-element" '
             'data-file-height="955" data-file-width="1350" decoding="async" height="177" '
             'loading="lazy" src="./Acetylcholine_Pathway.png" width="250"/><figcaption>Diagram of '
             'central cholinergic pathways that may be disrupted in PSP-RS. Degeneration in '
             'regions such as the pedunculopontine nucleus and nucleus basalis of Meynert can '
             'impair acetylcholine transmission to cortical and subcortical structures involved in '
             'memory, attention, and motor control.</figcaption></figure> <p>Individuals with '
             'PSP-RS also exhibit early neuronal loss in the pedunculopontine tegmentum (PPT), an '
             'area of the brain responsible for producing acetylcholine, a neurotransmitter '
             'involved in memory, learning, and motor function. The PPT sends cholinergic '
             'projections to several regions commonly affected by tau pathology in PSP, including '
             'the globus pallidus, substantia nigra, and pons. Degeneration of cholinergic neurons '
             'in the PPT can also disrupt the function of other neurotransmitter systems, '
             'particularly dopamine, which plays a key role in motor regulation. In PSP-RS, '
             'dopaminergic neuronal loss has been partly attributed to reduced cholinergic input '
             'to the substantia nigra, further contributing to motor impairments characteristic of '
             'the disease. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 '
             'id="Diagnosis">Diagnosis</h2></div> <p><a href="Magnetic_resonance_imaging" '
             'title="Magnetic resonance imaging">Magnetic resonance imaging</a> (MRI) is often '
             'used to diagnose PSP. MRI may show atrophy in the midbrain with preservation of the '
             '<a href="Pons" title="Pons">pons</a> giving a "hummingbird" sign. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Differential_diagnosis">Differential '
             'diagnosis</h3></div> <p>PSP is frequently misdiagnosed as <a '
             'href="Parkinson\'s_disease" title="Parkinson\'s disease">Parkinson\'s disease</a> '
             'because they both involve slowed movements and gait difficulty, with PSP being one '
             'of a collection of diseases referred to as <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Parkinson_plus_syndrome" title="Parkinson plus syndrome">Parkinson plus '
             "syndromes</a>. Both Parkinson's and PSP have an onset in late middle age and involve "
             'slowing and rigidity of movement. However, several distinguishing features exist. '
             "Tremor is very common with Parkinson's, but rare with PSP. Speech and swallowing "
             'difficulties are more common and severe with PSP and the abnormal eye movements of '
             'PSP are essentially absent with PD. A poor response to <a href="Levodopa" '
             'title="Levodopa">levodopa</a>, along with symmetrical onset can also help '
             "differentiate PSP from PD. </p><p>PSP can also be misdiagnosed as Alzheimer's "
             'disease because of the behavioral changes. </p><p><a '
             'href="Chronic_traumatic_encephalopathy" title="Chronic traumatic '
             'encephalopathy">Chronic traumatic encephalopathy</a> (CTE) shows many similarities '
             'with PSP, because both share the following attributes: </p> <ul><li>Accumulations of '
             'hyperphosphorylated tau protein in neurons or glial cells</li> <li>Accumulation of '
             'tau-<a class="mw-redirect" href="Immunoreactive" '
             'title="Immunoreactive">immunoreactive</a> <a href="Astrocyte" '
             'title="Astrocyte">astrocytes</a></li> <li>Involve the superficial <a '
             'class="mw-redirect" href="Cortical_layers" title="Cortical layers">cortical '
             'layers</a></li></ul> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="Types">Types</h3></div> <p>Based on the pathological findings in confirmed cases '
             'of PSP, it is divided into the following categories: </p> <ul><li>Classical '
             'Richardson syndrome (PSP-RS) </li> <li>PSP-parkinsonism (PSP-P) and PSP-pure '
             'akinesia with gait freezing (PSP-PAGF)</li> <li>Frontal PSP, PSP-corticobasal '
             'syndrome (PSP-CBS), PSP-behavioural variant of frontotemporal dementia (PSP-bvFTD) '
             'and PSP-progressive non-fluent aphasia (PSP-PNFA)</li> <li>PSP-C</li> <li>PSP '
             'induced by Annonaceae</li></ul> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="Diagnostic_differences_between_PSP_subtypes">Diagnostic differences between PSP '
             'subtypes</h3></div> <p>PSP-RS is the most common subtype of PSP. In PSP-P features '
             "of Parkinson's Disease overlap with the clinical presentation of PSP and follows a "
             'more benign course. In both PSP-P and PSP- PAGF distribution of abnormal tau is '
             'relatively restricted to the brain stem. Frontal PSP initially presents with '
             'behavioral and cognitive symptoms, with or without <a href="Ophthalmoparesis" '
             'title="Ophthalmoparesis">ophthalmoparesis</a> and then evolve into typical PSP. The '
             'phenotypes of PSP-P and PSP-PAGF are sometimes referred as the "brain stem" variants '
             'of PSP, as opposed to the "cortical" variants which present with predominant '
             'cortical features, including PSP-CBS, PSP-bvFTD, and PSP-PNFA. <a '
             'href="Cerebellar_ataxia" title="Cerebellar ataxia">Cerebellar ataxia</a> as the '
             'predominant early presenting feature is increasingly recognized as a very rare '
             'subtype of PSP (PSP-C) which is associated with severe neuronal loss with <a '
             'href="Gliosis" title="Gliosis">gliosis</a> and higher densities of coiled bodies in '
             'the cerebellar <a href="Dentate_nucleus" title="Dentate nucleus">dentate '
             'nucleus</a>. </p><p>The "humming-bird" imaging feature, indicative of midbrain '
             'degeneration, supports early diagnosis of both PSP-RS and PSP-P. One commonly used '
             'quantitative measure is the <b>midbrain-to-pons area ratio</b>, which is calculated '
             'by measuring the cross-sectional areas of the midbrain and pons on midsagittal MRI '
             'images.In healthy individuals, the midbrain and pons are proportionally sized, but '
             'in PSP-RS, the midbrain undergoes significant atrophy while the pons remains '
             'relatively spared. As a result, the ratio is markedly reduced in PSP-RS, '
             'distinguishing it from other neurodegenerative conditions such as Parkinson’s '
             'disease, multiple system atrophy (MSA), and even PSP-P, which typically exhibit less '
             'pronounced midbrain atrophy. To improve diagnostic specificity, the <b>magnetic '
             'resonance parkinsonism index (MRPI)</b> incorporates the midbrain-to-pons area ratio '
             'along with measurements of the widths of the middle and superior cerebellar '
             'peduncles. The MRPI has been shown to enhance discrimination between PSP-RS and '
             'other forms of atypical parkinsonism. Additional MRI findings associated with PSP-RS '
             'may include third ventricle enlargement, thinning of the superior cerebellar '
             'peduncles, and frontal lobe atrophy, all of which are consistent with the underlying '
             'tau pathology seen in the disease. Brain atrophy in PSP-RS is generally symmetrical, '
             'which may correspond to the bilateral presentation of motor and cognitive symptoms. '
             '</p><p>PSP-RS is thought to follow a grading system that reflects the progressive '
             'nature of the disease. In the early stages, atrophy affects midbrain structures such '
             'as the pons, and then progresses to involve the basal ganglia and pontine nuclei. In '
             'later stages, the dentate gyrus of the hippocampus and the cerebral cortices exhibit '
             'cell death and atrophy. PSP-RS is also associated with more widespread and severe '
             'cortical volume loss, particularly in the frontal pole, inferior frontal gyrus, '
             'globus pallidus, amygdala, and thalamus. Additionally, <b>diffusion tensor imaging '
             '(DTI)</b> studies show significant reductions in <b>fractional anisotropy,</b> a '
             'measure of white matter integrity, in the body of the corpus callosum, superior '
             'cerebellar peduncles, dentatorubrothalamic tract, and anterior thalamic radiation, '
             'indicating extensive white matter disruption in PSP-RS. </p><p>Positron emission '
             'tomography (PET) imaging has been used to detect tau accumulation in PSP-RS. The use '
             'of various tracers such as 18F-5105, 18F-FDDNP, 18F-THK523, 11C-PBB3 have shown '
             'increased binding in PSP-RS patients compared to healthy controls, reflecting '
             'elevated levels of 4R tau. Brain scans called SPECT have been used to help diagnose '
             'different types of Parkinson-like conditions, including PSP-RS. These scans show '
             'areas of reduced blood flow in the brain, but they aren’t specific enough to clearly '
             'tell PSP-RS apart from other similar conditions like PSP-P or corticobasal syndrome '
             '(CBS). Additionally, SPECT revealed greater perfusion deficits in PSP-RS than in '
             'PSP-P, although CBS showed the most frontal lobe deterioration overall. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading4"><h4 '
             'id="Differential_diagnosis_between_PSP-subtypes">Differential diagnosis between '
             'PSP-subtypes</h4></div> <p>The table below summarizes key clinical, pathological, '
             'and imaging differences between the main subtypes of PSP, including PSP-RS, PSP-P, '
             'and other variant forms. While all subtypes share the underlying 4-repeat tau '
             'pathology characteristic of PSP, they differ in symptom onset, disease progression, '
             'anatomical vulnerability, and response to treatment. Patients with the Richardson '
             'variant of PSP tend to have an upright posture or <a href="Lordosis" '
             'title="Lordosis">arched back</a>, as opposed to the <a href="Kyphosis" '
             'title="Kyphosis">stooped-forward</a> posture of other Parkinsonian disorders, '
             'although PSP-Parkinsonism (see below) can demonstrate a stooped posture. Early falls '
             'are also more common with PSP, especially with Richardson syndrome. </p> <table '
             'class="wikitable"> <tbody><tr> <th>Feature </th> <th>PSP-RS </th> <th>PSP-P </th> '
             '<th>Other PSP Subtypes (e.g., PSP-SL, PSP-CBS, PSP-F, PSP-PG) </th></tr> <tr> '
             '<td>Prevalence </td> <td>5-7 per 100,000 <p>24-54% of all PSP cases </p> </td> '
             '<td>13-45% of all PSP cases </td> <td>Less than 50% of PSP cases collectively '
             '</td></tr> <tr> <td>Onset and Progression </td> <td>Early falls, gaze palsy, early '
             'axial rigidity <p>Faster overall progression </p> </td> <td>Slower progression with '
             "symptoms mimicking Parkinson's disease </td> <td>Variable depending on the subtype "
             '</td></tr> <tr> <td>Motor Symptoms </td> <td>Early axial rigidity, bradykinesia, '
             'postural instability, rare tremor </td> <td>Asymmetric rigidity, resting tremor, '
             'better levodopa response </td> <td>PSP-PGF: progressive gait freezing <p>PSP-CBS: '
             'limb apraxia </p> </td></tr> <tr> <td>Ocular Symptoms </td> <td>Early vertical gaze '
             'palsy; eyelid apraxia; blurred vision </td> <td>Gaze palsy appears later or less '
             'severe </td> <td>Variable; may be absent or delayed onset </td></tr> <tr> '
             '<td>Cognitive Symptoms </td> <td>Prominent early executive dysfunction, apathy, '
             'emotional blunting </td> <td>Milder or later cognitive issues </td> <td>PSP-F: early '
             'behavioral symptoms such as apathy PSP-SL: language decline </td></tr> <tr> '
             '<td>Dopaminergic Response </td> <td>Poor or minimal response to levodopa </td> '
             '<td>Often initially responsive to levodopa </td> <td>Generally poor response '
             '</td></tr> <tr> <td>PSP-Tau Score (0–12) </td> <td>Typically &gt;7; widespread tau '
             'pathology </td> <td>≤5; limited tau burden </td> <td>Variable; usually less than '
             'PSP-RS </td></tr> <tr> <td>Affected Brain Regions (Path) </td> <td>Frontal pole, '
             'globus pallidus, thalamus, subthalamic nucleus, tectum </td> <td>Substantia nigra, '
             'subthalamic nucleus more selectively involved </td> <td>PSP-SL: perisylvian cortex; '
             'PSP-CBS: motor cortex </td></tr> <tr> <td>Disease Duration </td> <td>Around 5.9 '
             'years </td> <td>around 7-9 years </td> <td>Variable </td></tr></tbody></table> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Management">Management</h2></div> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Treatment">Treatment</h3></div> <figure '
             'class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><img class="mw-file-element" '
             'data-file-height="258" data-file-width="512" decoding="async" height="126" '
             'loading="lazy" src="./3%252C4-Dihydroxy-L-phenylalanin_(Levodopa).svg.png" '
             'width="250"/><figcaption>Chemical structure of levodopa (L-DOPA), a precursor to '
             'dopamine used in the treatment of Parkinsonian disorders, including PSP-RS. Levodopa '
             'crosses the blood–brain barrier and is converted to dopamine in the brain, '
             'temporarily alleviating motor symptoms.</figcaption></figure> <p>Management is only '
             'supportive is a <a href="Cure" title="Cure">cure</a> for PSP is known. PSP cases are '
             'often split into two subgroups, PSP-RS and PSP-P, where a short-term response to <a '
             'href="Levodopa" title="Levodopa">levodopa</a> can be obtained. <a href="Dyskinesia" '
             'title="Dyskinesia">Dyskinesia</a> is an occasional but rare complication of '
             'treatment. Other variants have been described. <a class="mw-redirect" href="Botox" '
             'title="Botox">Botox</a> can be used to treat neck <a href="Dystonia" '
             'title="Dystonia">dystonia</a> and <a href="Blepharospasm" '
             'title="Blepharospasm">blepharospasm</a>, but this can aggravate <a href="Dysphagia" '
             'title="Dysphagia">dysphagia</a>. </p><p>Two studies have suggested that <a '
             'href="Rivastigmine" title="Rivastigmine">rivastigmine</a> may help with cognitive '
             'aspects, but the authors of both studies have suggested that larger studies are '
             'needed. There is some evidence from small-scale studies that the hypnotic <a '
             'href="Zolpidem" title="Zolpidem">zolpidem</a> may improve motor function and eye '
             'movements. </p><p>Current clinical trials focus on disease modifying treatments but '
             'there are currently no FDA approved disease modifying treatments for PSP-RS. Recent '
             'trials have focused on therapies targeting the 4R tau accumulation in the brain of '
             'those with PSP-RS. These therapies include microtubule-stabilizing agents, tau '
             'gain-of-function therapies, and monoclonal antibodies. These therapies are currently '
             'being tested in clinical trials. </p> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="Rehabilitation">Rehabilitation</h3></div> <p>Patients with PSP usually seek or '
             'are referred to <a href="Occupational_therapy" title="Occupational '
             'therapy">occupational therapy</a>, speech-language pathology for motor speech '
             'changes (typically a spastic-ataxic <a href="Dysarthria" '
             'title="Dysarthria">dysarthria</a>), and physical therapy for balance and gait '
             'problems with reports of frequent falls. There has been research in the use of '
             'robot-assisted gait training. Evidence-based approaches to rehabilitation in PSP are '
             'lacking and, currently the majority of research on the subject consists of case '
             'reports involving only a small number of patients. </p><p>Case reports of '
             'rehabilitation programs for patients with PSP generally include limb-coordination '
             'activities, tilt-board balancing, gait training, strength training with progressive '
             'resistive exercises, and isokinetic exercises and stretching of the neck muscles. '
             'While some case reports suggest that physiotherapy can offer improvements in balance '
             'and gait of patients with PSP, the results cannot be generalized across all PSP '
             'patients, as each case report followed only one or two patients. The observations '
             'made from these case studies can be useful however, in helping to guide future '
             'research concerning the effectiveness of balance and gait training programs in the '
             'management of PSP. </p><p>Individuals with PSP are often referred to occupational '
             'therapists to help manage their condition and to help enhance their independence. '
             'This may include being taught to use <a href="Mobility_aid" title="Mobility '
             'aid">mobility aids</a>. Due to their tendency to fall backwards, the use of a '
             'walker, particularly one that can be weighted in the front, is recommended instead '
             "of a cane. The use of an appropriate mobility aid helps to decrease the individual's "
             'risk of falls and makes them safer to ambulate independently in the community. Due '
             'to their balance problems and irregular movements, individuals need to spend time '
             'learning how to safely transfer in their homes and in the community. This may '
             'include rising from and sitting in chairs safely. </p><p>Due to the progressive '
             'nature of this disease, all individuals eventually lose their ability to walk and '
             'will need to progress to using a wheelchair. Severe <a href="Dysphagia" '
             'title="Dysphagia">dysphagia</a> often follows, and at this point <a href="Death" '
             'title="Death">death</a> is often a matter of months. </p> <div class="mw-heading '
             'mw-heading2"><h2 id="Prognosis">Prognosis</h2></div> <p>No effective treatment or '
             'cure has been found for PSP, although some of the <a class="mw-redirect" '
             'href="Symptom" title="Symptom">symptoms</a> can respond to nonspecific measures. The '
             'poor prognosis is predominantly attributed to the serious impact this condition has '
             'on the quality of life. The average age at symptoms onset is 63 and survival from '
             'onset averages seven years with a wide variance. <a href="Pneumonia" '
             'title="Pneumonia">Pneumonia</a> is a frequent cause of death, often caused by '
             'accidental aspiration of food particles. </p> <div class="mw-heading '
             'mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2></div> <p>In 1877, Charcot described a '
             '40-year-old woman who had rigid-akinetic parkinsonism, neck dystonia, dysarthria, '
             'and eye-movement problems. In 1951, Chavany and others reported the clinical and '
             'pathologic features of a 50-year-old man with a rigid and akinetic form of '
             'parkinsonism with postural instability, neck dystonia, dysarthria, and staring gaze. '
             'In 1974, Albert and colleagues first described the unique frontal lobe cognitive '
             'changes of progressive supranuclear palsy—apathy, loss of spontaneity, slowing of '
             'thought processes, and loss of <a href="Executive_functions" title="Executive '
             'functions">executive functions</a>. </p><p>Between 1877 and 1963, 22 well-documented '
             'case reports of PSP, although not described as a distinct disorder, had been '
             'identified in the literature of neurology. Progressive supranuclear palsy was first '
             'described as a distinct disorder by neurologists John Steele, John Richardson, and '
             'Jerzy Olszewski in 1963. They recognized the same clinical syndrome in eight '
             'patients, and described the autopsy findings in six of them. </p> <div '
             'class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Society_and_culture">Society and '
             'culture</h2></div> <p>There are several organizations around the world that support '
             'PSP patients and the research into PSP and related diseases, such as <a '
             'href="Corticobasal_degeneration" title="Corticobasal degeneration">corticobasal '
             'degeneration</a> (CBD) and <a href="Multiple_system_atrophy" title="Multiple system '
             'atrophy">multiple system atrophy</a> (MSA). </p> <ul><li>Canada: PSP Society of '
             'Canada, a federally registered non-profit organization which serves patients and '
             'families dealing with PSP, CBD and MSA, set up in 2017 through the help of CurePSP '
             'in the USA</li> <li>France: Association PSP France, a nonprofit patient association '
             'set up in 1996 through the help of PSPA in the UK. It also gives support to French '
             'speaking patients in Quebec, Morocco, Algeria, Belgium and Lebanon</li> <li> UK: '
             'PSPA, a national charity for information, patient support and research of PSP and '
             'CBD, set up in 1994</li> <li>Ireland: PSPAI, an organization which aims to increase '
             'public awareness of PSP</li> <li>US: CurePSP, a nonprofit organization for promoting '
             'awareness, care and research of PSP, CBD, MSA "and other prime of life '
             'neurodegenerative diseases"</li></ul> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 '
             'id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</h3></div> <p>In the 2020 American '
             "musical comedy-drama television series, <i>Zoey's Extraordinary Playlist</i>, the "
             "title character's father (Mitch Clarke, played by Peter Gallagher) has PSP. </p> "
             '<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Notable_cases">Notable cases</h3></div> '
             '<ul><li>Dudley Moore (1935–2002)\xa0: English actor, comedian, musician and '
             'composer</li> <li>Peter Sarstedt (1941-2017)\xa0: English singer and songwriter</li> '
             '<li>Linda Ronstadt (b. 1946)\xa0: American singer</li> <li>Jennifer Wexton (b. '
             '1968)\xa0: former US Representative</li> <li>Phyllis Frelich (1944–2014)\xa0: Tony '
             'Award-winning actor</li> <li>Lee Wei Ling (1955–2024)\xa0: Singaporean '
             'neurologist</li> <li>Richard Rainwater (1944-2015)\xa0: American investor and '
             'philanthropist</li> <li>Jeff Golub (1955-2015)\xa0: American Rock and Jazz guitar '
             'musician</li></ul> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="See_also">See '
             'also</h2></div> <ul><li><a href="Lytico-bodig_disease" title="Lytico-bodig '
             'disease">Lytico-bodig disease</a> (Parkinsonism-Dementia Complex of Guam)</li> '
             '<li>Annonacin</li></ul> <div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 '
             'id="References">References</h2></div> <div class="reflist"> <div '
             'class="mw-references-wrap mw-references-columns"><ol class="references"> <li '
